Video conferencing technology has gained immense popularity in recent years. Some of the advantages of VMC compared to text-based communication include the exchange of non-verbal feedback and attitudinal cues, and gestural forms for emphasis and elaboration[(Isaacs and Tang, 1994](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B29); [Doherty-Sneddon et al., 1997](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B18)). For example, even when there are interruptions in the audio channel, the visual channel can provide important context for interpreting them[(Isaacs and Tang, 1994](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B29)). However, compared to face-to-face communication, there are still many limitations. When communicating in groups, such as in a classroom setting, turn-taking and floor management are difficult because they rely on accurate gaze direction judgment, which is generally absent in VMC platforms[(Doherty-Sneddon et al., 1997)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B18). For the same reason, assisting with tasks is also difficult[(Neale et al., 1998](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B45)). Without additional channels, side conversations are impossible, and pointing to and manipulating real shared objects can be cumbersome[(Neale et al., 1998](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B45); [Nardi and Whittaker, 2002](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B43)). Recently, there have also been studies investigating the effectiveness of VMC systems like Zoom and their impact on users during prolonged use. Serhan investigated students' perceptions of using Zoom in remote learning and its impact on learning and engagement compared to face-to-face learning[(Serhan, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B54)). The survey results indicated that students had negative attitudes towards using Zoom and perceived it as having a negative impact on their learning experience and motivation. Students cited flexibility as the biggest disadvantage of using Zoom for learning, suggesting that more flexible tools like Mozilla Hub could complement Zoom in learning environments. However, in terms of accessibility, VMC platforms were still preferred over avatar-based platforms. While most VMC platforms support screen reading and the inclusion of sign language interpreters, the accessibility aspects of MH still require improvement[(Usi, 2021](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B58)).

**2.3. Avatar-Based Communication**

One of the most challenging problems faced by CMC platforms is giving participants the impression that they are sharing the same virtual space[(Vilhjälmsson, 2003)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B62). Avatar-based platforms have the ability to draw participants into a shared virtual environment[(Vilhjálmsson, 2003](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B62)). The first multi-user graphical online world was Habitat, developed around 1985[(Damer et al., 1997](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B13)). Habitat users could gather in a virtual town, chat, trade virtual props, play games, and solve quests. Users could move their avatars using cursor keys and communicate with other users only by typing short messages. Voice was not supported. Since Habitat, avatar-based systems have been used for a variety of purposes, from casual chat and games to military training simulations and online classrooms. For example, Second Life is an early avatar-based system where players design the world, objects, and actions themselves. Second Life integrates 3D modeling tools and a powerful scripting language[(Delwiche, 2006](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B15)). Mayrath et al. applied Second Life to a pilot case study integrating it into two semesters of English classes at a large university[(Mayrath et al., 2007](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B37)). This paper, similar to our research, focuses on describing the process of implementing avatar-based tools in the classroom and conducting a formative evaluation of students' related experiences. However, it focuses on identifying the main instructional design aspects of learning activities in virtual environments, and provides little coverage on how to design virtual spaces or on students' behavior and communication patterns within virtual environments.

**2.4. Social VR Platforms in Education**

Several studies have investigated aspects of social interaction in shared online 3D environments and VR. DIVE is a distributed interactive virtual environment platform designed to scale to accommodate a large number of concurrent participants [(Frécon and Stenius, 1998)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B22). In this study, the authors describe the network software architecture and strategies adopted to achieve scalability while maintaining a high level of interaction among users. Other studies have investigated immersive group telepresence based on projection-based multi-user setups that provide perspective-correct stereoscopic images to multiple users [(Beck et al., 2013)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B5), while others have focused on developing distributed multi-user and multi-platform systems for collaborative visualization [(Khadka et al., 2018)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B32). This approach is similar in features to Mozilla Hubs. It supports system access across multiple platforms, such as desktops, head-mounted displays, or smartphones. Other researchers have explored aspects of social interaction in VR. Researchers interviewed industry professionals from various social VR applications to explore how platforms structure, support, shape, and constrain social interactions [(McVeigh-Schultz et al., 2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B38). Other researchers also interviewed industry professionals to investigate current design practices and reflect on design approaches characterizing avatars and avatar systems [(Kolesnichenko et al., 2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B34). Our approach differs because it focused on observing and analyzing graduate and undergraduate students in a classroom setting. As part of a research effort closer to our approach, researchers developed a custom Mozilla Hub to analyze several aspects, such as group formation and behavior in virtual classrooms. They mostly adopted automated data collection methods (e.g., proxemics) and observed users for short periods in a workshop setting [(Williamson et al., 2021)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B71). Online 3D environments can be a medium for discussion for those who can remotely participate in classroom lectures and those who cannot physically attend [(Isaacs and Tang, 1997)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B30)). Furthermore, these environments enable various activities, experiments, and explorations to be conducted in the virtual world that might be too costly, dangerous, or impossible in the physical world [(Eiris et al., 2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B19); [Wen and Gheisari, 2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B67)). The use of avatars in learning environments has been investigated in this context. The University of Colorado-Boulder offered an online business computing course utilizing various CMC tools, including the web, video conferencing, and avatar-based shared online virtual environments. This environment essentially provided a virtual campus where students could access resources located in various buildings. Walkways and parks next to these virtual buildings naturally grouped students studying related topics and provided opportunities for discussion and unexpected encounters [(Dickey, 1999)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B16)). However, these platforms were resource-intensive, required specialized software, and needed relatively expensive machines to run with adequate performance. Development for these was the domain of computer science professionals, and existing tools did not facilitate user-centered content creation. Researchers have investigated several aspects of educational social VR platforms [(Pan et al., 2006)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B48); [Scavarelli et al., 2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B53); [Vergara et al., 2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B60); [Bredikhina et al., 2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B10); [Holt et al., 2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B27)). Pan et al. analyzed virtual learning environments (VLEs) in both virtual and augmented reality. They show that VLEs can enhance learners' understanding of specific events in the learning environment, motivate them, and stimulate them [(Pan et al., 2006)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B48)). Scavarelli et al. proposed a framework for accessible and social VR environments in education [(Scavarelli et al., 2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B53)). This study is significant in that it introduced elements of computer-supported collaborative learning into the development of socio-educational VR systems. The work by Scavarelli et al. complements our work to some extent. While their study provides guidelines for building accessible social VR platforms for learning, our study focuses on providing guidelines for creating virtual environments within such platforms. For example, Scavarelli et al. suggest that accessible social VR platforms should allow multiple users to collaborate and scale to various group sizes, including different forms of communication such as voice and gestures. They also suggest that avatars should be customizable by users. However, these guidelines do not provide information on how virtual spaces should be constructed to facilitate communication, collaboration, or team formation, which is the focus of this study. Nevertheless, it is noteworthy that the Mozilla Hubs platform adopted for this investigation follows the guidelines proposed by Scavarelli et al.

Other researchers have begun to investigate the use of avatars and Mozilla Hubs in educational settings. [Bredikhina et al. (2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B10) described the design and procedures for live workshops for children in Mozilla Hubs. They developed a step-by-step method called 'Sugoroku' for spatial design and audio calculation, which proved efficient in children's workshops. Other researchers, inspired by the limitations of isolation caused by the COVID-19 pandemic, explored applying Mozilla Hubs to science conferences and classroom poster sessions. [Holt et al. (2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B27) described their findings from conducting end-of-semester virtual poster sessions via Mozilla Hubs. They collected qualitative data and shared the advantages and disadvantages of the experience as perceived by students, instructors, and external observers. To our knowledge, our study differs and is unique in its objectives and duration. We adopted Mozilla Hubs as an integral part of undergraduate and graduate courses, allowing students to be repeatedly exposed to a virtual environment over the course of a semester. We also aimed to investigate and build virtual rooms that foster interaction, communication, and team building. We hope to provide a set of guidelines for developing such environments, allowing them to be generalized to other classes and subjects beyond virtual reality education.

**3. VR4SG Course**

VR4SG[(VRF, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B64)) is a course that teaches the application of immersive technologies to social good topics. The course taught virtual reality, augmented reality, and mixed reality, using a blended approach of theory and practice by connecting groups of aspiring problem-solvers (students regardless of major or year) with groups of seekers (researchers, innovators, entrepreneurs) looking for solutions to real-world social good problems. One of the key features of the VR4SG course is that students apply Lean and Agile principles[(Plenert, 2011](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B49); [Williams, 2012](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B70)). Thus, teams develop projects with the guiding principle of learning VR concepts and delivering value efficiently by implementing short, iterative improvement cycles for project deliverables. Another feature of the course is that students form their own cross-functional teams and work on projects throughout the semester. In previous years, course projects implemented fully immersive virtual reality experiences built with the Unity3D game engine[(Jerald et al., 2014)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B31)). Most projects required virtual reality head-mounted displays (HMDs). A limited number of HMDs were provided, and students had to organize themselves to develop projects using shared equipment. Students spent a significant amount of time learning how to develop with Unity3D compared to the effort they put into designing and conceptualizing virtual environments. In the last class of the semester, all groups presented the virtual environments they had developed over the semester to seekers and the general public in an in-person format.

**3.1. Moving to Remote**

In the Fall 2020 semester, the VR4SG course was offered once again. This time, due to quarantine requirements imposed by the pandemic, the course was conducted online. Transitioning the VR4SG course to an online format presented several challenges. One challenge was how to foster student collaboration and teamwork. Additionally, due to limited contact in the online format, we had to consider how to support students in forming their own teams. Another challenge was technical. Typically, teams develop fully immersive VR experiences based on HMDs using the Unity3D platform and rely on group meetings to use shared spaces and equipment, none of which were available this semester. Furthermore, Unity3D does not inherently support remote collaboration, making it impractical for remote learning. To successfully conduct the class under these constraints, new approaches to teaching, development, and collaboration had to be implemented.

**3.1.1. Defining the New Course Format**

To conduct the VR4SG course online, we chose Zoom, a video conferencing platform. The mosaic structure showing camera feeds of many students (49 in this case) provides some aspects of real in-person communication, such as non-verbal feedback and attitudinal cues, suitable for online lectures. However, while Zoom can efficiently support instructor presentations, it has limitations that significantly impact group activities and teamwork in an educational setting[(Serhan, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B54)). The lack of spatial interaction and mobility clearly demonstrated that typical group activities do not directly translate or are not effective on the Zoom platform. Indeed, the lack of direct interaction among students meant that guiding them to form their own teams for the course's main project was a major challenge to overcome.

**3.1.2. Choosing Mozilla Hubs**

To overcome the limitations of Zoom and typical video conferencing platforms, we sought to introduce an avatar-based communication (AMC) platform that could complement the Zoom-based classes and serve as a primary development tool for students. An AMC platform could provide a 3D virtual shared space where students could meet and work in groups, replicating several aspects of real teamwork. We looked for an avatar-based platform where students could collaboratively design and build immersive environments. Additionally, we sought a platform that would offer students the option to develop both fully immersive experiences (e.g., those requiring HMDs) and experiences accessible via desktops or other devices without VR-specific equipment, and one that would reduce complexity by not requiring users to install specific software on their devices. We also aimed for an application focused on end-user content creation. After reviewing and testing several AMC platforms[(Alt, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B1); [Hub, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B28); [VRC, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B63)), we found that Mozilla Hubs (MH) met most, if not all, of our requirements, while other platforms fell short in several aspects. For example, AltSpace VR[(Alt, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B1)) requires users to download an application and does not focus on end-user content creation. VRChat([VRC, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B63)) requires Unity3D for users to create content and environments. Mozilla Hubs (MH) is an AMC platform designed for all headsets and browsers. It is open-source and focuses on user-generated content creation. MH allows groups of up to 24 students to easily share virtual rooms, interact, and collaborate on group activities simultaneously. The MH platform is web-based, requiring no separate software installation beyond an internet browser, and runs on a variety of devices, from HMDs to laptops, desktops, and smartphones. Users can embody and interact with avatars that are easy to create and customize. Users can use their own names or pseudonyms for their avatars. Content creation is done by dragging and dropping content such as 3D objects, PDF files, multimedia content, or web pages into the virtual environment, or by selecting content from a built-in library. MH supports spatial audio, so sounds fade or grow louder depending on the distance between the avatar and the source, making sounds in the room feel realistic. To enhance user interaction and provide visual feedback, the avatar's head moves when the user controlling it speaks. Users can interact via text in addition to voice, but this feature is not available when accessing MH with an HMD. However, MH has some limitations compared to Unity3D when it comes to developing immersive virtual environments. For example, MH does not allow the implementation of scripts (code that automates actions), custom graphical user interfaces, or character animations.

***Translator's Note: Animations and characters created using Blender can be inserted. Interactive assets are currently under development by the Mozilla Hubs project group as of August 2023, ***[***already implementable at the source level - development example***](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ySINGYYsQQ8)*** and are expected to be updated in the near future.***

Nevertheless, thanks to its ease of use, portability, and speed in creating and sharing social virtual environments, students were able to reduce the time and effort spent dealing with development and technical issues (compared to Unity3D). By reducing the time spent on technical aspects, students could focus more effort on the conceptualization and design of their projects, which significantly altered and potentially improved the quality of their creations. For these reasons, the MH platform was chosen to complement Zoom when teaching VR4SG classes remotely.

**4. Preparing Students**

After selecting MH, instructors followed best practice guidelines suggested by Mayrath et al. to educate and support students using the AMC platform [(Mayrath et al., 2007)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B37), and developed three tutorials covering various aspects of MH. The goal was to concisely and effectively introduce Mozilla Hubs to students, enabling them to quickly make the most of the platform. To this end, three hands-on tutorial sessions (delivered via Zoom and Mozilla Hubs) were developed and provided to students. The first tutorial covered basic guidelines for using MH. The second tutorial demonstrated how to create advanced MH rooms. The third tutorial presented several approaches related to avatar creation and customization.

**4.1. Basic Mozilla Hubs Tutorial**

The first tutorial provided to students combined a Zoom presentation with hands-on activities in MH. After a brief review of MH features on Zoom, students were guided to join an MH room. In the MH part, students explored a room with five stations and performed various activities. Students practiced how to access the platform, basic controls for navigation, and how to add content to a room. They practiced walking, running, flying, and customizing the space by adding various types of content, including 3D models and persistent text messages. Students were also instructed on how to set their names and choose avatars. [Figure 1](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F1) shows the basic MH tutorial room.

{{IMG:1}}

**Figure 1**. Mozilla Hubs tutorial room. The five-stage tutorial room, containing educational information and activities, was developed to provide students with basic skills to navigate and use the Mozilla Hubs platform.

The tutorial room was built as a large, open environment with no walls and minimal details, specifically designed for this purpose. To accommodate all students, two identical classrooms were created. Students were randomly and equally assigned to each classroom (classroom link: [https://hubs.mozilla.com/bRpoAXc/basic-tutorial-mozilla-hubs-a)](https://hubs.mozilla.com/bRpoAXc/basic-tutorial-mozilla-hubs-a)). By adopting a minimalist design with no walls and few 3D models or details, the goal was to develop a room that did not require excessive computing resources from students' hardware, thereby providing the best possible experience for all students. To better understand student behavior in MH, in addition to observing student interactions in the tutorial room,

Observation of students using the basic tutorial revealed that they were unable to accurately place content in the room. This fact could potentially limit the complexity and quality of the content students could create. To address this limitation, the second tutorial focused on enabling students to develop sophisticated MH rooms and precisely adjust the size and position of objects within the environment.

Mozilla Spoke[(Spo, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B56)) is a world-building tool for Mozilla Hubs, focusing on facilitating the creation of 3D virtual spaces for the Mozilla Hubs platform. Mozilla Spoke is fully integrated with MH, and environments created in Mozilla Spoke can be automatically published to MH. Its user interface shares many similarities with other existing 3D creation tools (e.g., Unity3D) and comes with a large set of predefined assets such as floor elements, walls, doors, and windows. Importantly, it adopts a parametric approach to object orientation, position, and scaling (see [Figure 2](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F2)). This allows users to numerically determine and precisely define these properties. Similar to previous tutorials, the Mozilla Spoke tutorial session was delivered using a combination of Zoom and Mozilla Spoke. Users were invited to complete activities and follow along using Mozilla Spoke during the tutorial.

{{IMG:2}}
                            **Figure 2**. Mozilla Spoke building tool. Mozilla Spoke allows for easy creation of virtual environments with precise control over the position, rotation, and scale of objects in Mozilla Hubs rooms. The image details some of the parametric controls.

**4.3. Customizing Avatars**

Another drawback observed in the basic tutorial was the limited ability for students to customize their avatars when relying on the existing MH avatar library. Providing students with additional tools to customize their avatars was essential, as avatars can influence a user's experience in 3D virtual social spaces in several ways ([Nowak and Fox, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B46)). For instance, it has been demonstrated that avatar characteristics can enhance or limit people's ability for self-expression, as well as influence how people interact with each other in digital environments ([Nowak and Fox, 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B46)). Therefore, a tutorial session was provided to students, emphasizing the importance of avatars and demonstrating various approaches to creating and customizing them. Similar to previous tutorial sessions, this tutorial was delivered on Zoom, accompanied by hands-on activities for students in MH. The three strategies presented to students for creating and customizing avatars were:

- **Changing an avatar's skin with Quilt**. Quilt[(Qui, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B51)) is a simple web-based avatar editor for MH that allows for easy design of new skins for avatars. Quilt can apply regular image files as avatar skins, preview them, and export them to MH. While it does not allow for changing the avatar's shape, the ability to re-skin significantly increases the avatar's customization possibilities. [Figure 3B](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F3) shows an avatar re-skinned with Quilt.
- **Changing an avatar's shape**. To provide students with more options for customizing avatars, training was conducted on how to create 3D models for avatars. MH uses the GLTF 2.0[(glT, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B23)) file format for rendering avatars. Students were taught how to create 3D models for avatars using open-source 3D modeling tools like Blender[(Ble, 2020)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B6) and shown how to download 3D models from existing repositories[(Pol, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B50); [Ske, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B55)). [Figure 3C](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F3) shows an avatar imported from [Sketchfab.com](https://www.sketchfab.com/) ([Ske, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B55)).
- **Generating avatars based on user photos**. The most popular avatar creation tool among students was Readyplayer.me[(Rea, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B52)). Readyplayer.me automatically generates avatars based on an image taken of the user's face. Users can customize various aspects of their new avatar. Customizations include facial features like eye and hair color, as well as clothing colors and styles. One limiting factor is that avatars generated with this tool consist only of the head and upper body, not the full body of the user. However, the ability to automatically generate an avatar resembling the user's face offers users great flexibility in creating personalized avatars. [Figure 3D](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F3) shows an avatar generated with Readyplayer.me.

***Translator's Note: It appears that Quilt is currently not in service. Instead, there are better methods not included in this paper, such as using the ***[***hackweek avatar maker***](https://mozilla.github.io/hackweek-avatar-maker/)*** and ***[***qt-mkr***](https://www.qt-mkr.com/)***. It's possible that Quilt refers to qt-mkr. As mentioned in another post, Believer will also be open-sourcing a full-body avatar maker similar to hackweek.***

{{IMG:3}}
                                
                            **Figure 3. Mozilla Hubs Avatars. (A) Default Mozilla Hubs avatar. (B) Avatar customized for the VR4SG course using the Quilt tool. (C) Custom model of an avatar imported from** [**Sketchfab.com**](https://www.sketchfab.com/) [**(Ske, 2020**](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B55)**). (D) Avatar generated from a user's photo using Ready Player Me** [**(Rea, 2020**](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B52)**). (E,F) Other avatars chosen by VR4SG course students.**

In the activities that followed this tutorial, students were encouraged to customize their avatars. However, avatar customization was not strictly required. Students were free to choose any avatar to explore the MH rooms. [Figures 3E, F](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F3) show some of the avatars chosen by the students. We discuss students' avatar choices in more detail in the next section.

**5. Mozilla Hubs Room Development**

After training students on how to use the MH platform, we developed three rooms as activities to complement the Zoom lectures. These rooms were intended to help students become more familiar with the MH platform and to help instructors understand how students behave in a virtual environment.

**5.1. Room1 - United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (UNSDG) Room**

The United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (UNSDG) room was designed to allow each student to create content advocating for one of their chosen SDGs. Five identical rooms were created to support all students. The SDGs are 17 goals designed as a blueprint to achieve a better and more sustainable future for all [(ARE, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B3)). The SDGs were set by the United Nations in 2015 and aim to be achieved by 2030. Examples of UN Sustainable Development Goals include No Poverty, Zero Hunger, Good Health and Well-being, and Quality Education [(ARE, 2020](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B3)). These goals align with the VR4SG course objectives and provide relevant real-world topics for students to work on. A link to one of the UNSDG rooms is provided here: [https://hubs.mozilla.com/Rm7wCxq/group-e-activity-1-and-2.](https://hubs.mozilla.com/Rm7wCxq/group-e-activity-1-and-2)**​**

**5.1.1. Objectives and Challenges**

Each student was instructed to choose one of the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals and fill a space in the MH room with content explaining that topic. The students' goal was to not only raise visitors' awareness of their chosen issue but also to encourage visitors to donate to that issue. Students were guided to find real organizations working for their chosen goal so that actual donations could be made. The objective of this class activity was to have a real-world impact. Students were given one week to develop content for their space. The instructors selected the top five projects and encouraged students to vote. To do this, all students were given 10 virtual coins that they could use to vote for their preferred project. Ultimately, the room with the most votes received recognition from the instructors and additional credit. This motivated students to do their best in project development. Through the UNSDG room, our goal was not only to observe and collect data to understand how to create virtual rooms that could foster collaboration among students but also to provide students with a virtual space that motivated them to interact and communicate. To this end, we created the UNSDG room shown in [Figure 4](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F4).

{{IMG:4}}
                                
                            **Figure 4**. United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (UNSDG) Room. **(A)** Instructions and information about the UNSDGs are displayed on the wall in the center of the room. **(B)** Overall layout of the room. Each room had 10 circular spaces that students could fill with content related to the UN Sustainable Development Goal they chose to work on. **(C)** A space filled by one of the students. The orange square shows a misaligned website in the room, highlighting the students' difficulty in accurately placing objects in MH.

**5.1.2. Room Design**

The UNSDG room design is based on 11 interconnected open spaces, delineated by circles on the floor. The room has one large central space, surrounded by 10 circular spaces. The central space displays information about each UNSDG and instructions for students (e.g., how to find their own space). Each space around the central area is numbered, and each student is assigned a number before entering the room. Similar to the basic tutorial room, we decided to design a room with minimal detail to allow users to add their own content without degrading the user experience (which would increase the complexity of the room and require more computing resources). To foster collaboration and create a sense of sharing space with others, we arranged the individual spaces in a circular pattern. This design allowed each student to always see each other. We expected this to promote collaboration and interaction among students. To understand students' behavior and impressions of this room, in addition to observing them, we conducted a survey after the activity.

**5.1.3. Student Survey Responses**

Students who agreed to participate in the initial survey (related to the basic tutorial described in Section 4.1) were also invited to participate in a subsequent survey. However, only 15 of the initial 19 students chose to respond to this survey. All participants reported accessing the UNSDG room with a desktop or laptop. Students were asked about the time they spent in the room and their impressions of the room's size and level of detail. More than half of the participants (60%) reported spending between 10 and 20 minutes, 3 (20%) between 20 and 30 minutes, and the remaining 3 spent more than 40 minutes. When asked about the room size, 11 (73.3%) participants responded that the room was large or too large, while the remaining participants responded that the room size was appropriate. The room size was also mentioned in responses about what participants liked least about the room. One-third of participants stated that the room size was too large. One participant commented, "*There was too much wasted space between each content area, making it take too long to move from one area to another*." Another student simply stated, "*It was too big*." Regarding the level of detail in the room, students mentioned that the room was "too simple," "too plain," and "empty."

**5.1.4. Researchers' Observations**

Two researchers from our group observed students 'in the wild'[(Chamberlain et al., 2012)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B11) in the room described in this manuscript. The researchers observed from a distance to avoid influencing the students' behavior. Observations of the students revealed that they could easily understand the room's layout and find their own workspaces. Although a simple open-space design without walls was adopted, circles on the floor delineated multiple work areas, helping students understand and navigate the room. One positive aspect of this room design was the observation that some students moved around the room, looking at other students' work. Additionally, we observed moments where students interacted with each other as they progressed with their projects, which was also a positive aspect of this room. However, there were rarely more than three students using the same room, and in those moments, students rarely conversed or interacted with each other in any way. We hypothesized that three factors were at play. First, to accommodate all 49 students in the VR4SG class, we decided to create five identical UNSDG rooms, each with 10 individual workspaces. This setup provided an opportunity for up to 10 students to see and interact with each other as they worked on their projects. Second, because each student had to choose one of the 17 UNSDGs, there were few students working on the same topic, which did not foster collaboration or idea exchange. Finally, students were given a week to develop the room, but there was no set schedule for project progress. While this approach offered great flexibility, it did not encourage students to work simultaneously, so not many students encountered each other during that period. Additionally, the fact that student spaces only had numbers and no names or other identifying information did not help students connect with each other.

To more effectively foster collaboration and interaction among students, the following activity aimed to develop a task and an MH room that adhered to four principles​1.
1.  **Share a common goal**. All students should feel a sense of commonality, sharing the same goal, which necessitates communication and collaboration.
2.  **Participate simultaneously**. All students must participate in the activity at the same time for everyone to meet and interact.
3.  **Develop smaller classrooms**. The developed classrooms should be designed to accommodate as many students as possible without being too large.
4.  **Develop higher quality rooms**. Based on student feedback, a higher quality environment should be created. The following describes the room and activity developed according to these principles.​

**5.2. Room2 - Pioneers of VR**

VR4SG is an introductory course to virtual reality. Therefore, one of the topics covered is the history and evolution of virtual reality (VR). To complement Zoom lectures covering various equipment, researchers, and inventors who influenced the field of VR research, the 'Pioneers of VR' classroom was developed. In this MH room, seven short videos were provided to students. These videos explain the history of influential early VR researchers and their contributions, such as Ivan Sutherland's "Ultimate Display"[(Sutherland, 1965](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B57)) and Morton Heilig's "Sensorama"[(Heilig, 1962](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B24)), as well as later works like the "Data Glove"[(Foley, 1987](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B21)) developed around 1987. The link to access the room is: [https://hubs.mozilla.com/9kHLTMK/pioneers-of-vr-2021.](https://hubs.mozilla.com/9kHLTMK/pioneers-of-vr-2021)​

**5.2.1. Goals and Tasks**

The purpose of this room was to observe student behavior and communication in a virtual space where students primarily consumed content. After the instructor delivered a lecture on the Zoom platform, students had 40 minutes to enter one of two identical VR rooms and explore the content. Students were instructed to watch all videos and interact within the virtual space.​

**5.2.2. Room Design**

According to UNSDG room feedback, in this room, all students had a shared goal (watching videos), and the activity took place at a specific time for a short duration (40 minutes). The design of this classroom is very different from the previous one (see [Figure 4](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F4)). The room developed for the previous activity was minimalist (with few details) and based on a large open space. For this activity, we created a small enclosed room with walls, doors, and lighting to simulate the interior of a building, and the virtual environment was more complex and detailed. Art galleries and museums inspired the room's design. Museums could be a useful metaphor as they are typically well-designed, well-lit, and rich social spaces. Furthermore, the museum metaphor aligns with the historical aspect of the presented videos and alludes to the task of watching the videos provided to students in this space. We hypothesized that this design language would reduce the emptiness and simplicity students felt about the previous classroom while encouraging communication and interaction among students within the classroom. The room consisted of two adjacent spaces. The first space was a small square room with sofas, VR equipment, and a large wall-mounted display. The display showed slides with task instructions and explained how the room was organized. Opening a door to the left of this room led to a larger L-shaped space, where seven videos were placed on the walls. To the left of each video, a small poster with the creator's name, title, and number was affixed, allowing students to easily identify each video. Each video was illuminated by one or more spotlights to draw students' attention. The heuristic adopted to define the room size was that 24 standard-sized Mozilla Hub avatars should comfortably fit in the room. This means, generally, avatars should be able to maintain a distance of at least one avatar length from each other. The room's design is shown in [Figure 5](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F5).

{{IMG:5}}
                                
**Figure 5**. Pioneers of VR classroom. **(A)** Classroom with instructions for students. **(B)** L-shaped room layout with seven videos on influential works in the field of virtual reality. **(C)** Detailed view of video number 1.

**​**

**5.2.3. Student Survey Responses**

Similar to the previous room, all participants reported accessing the Pioneers of VR room via a desktop or laptop. The videos in the room were, on average, 3 minutes long. Since there were 7 videos, it took just over 21 minutes to watch all of them. Of the 16 respondents, 6 (37.5%) reported spending between 10 and 20 minutes, 9 (56%) between 20 and 30 minutes, and only 1 (6%) spent more than 50 minutes in the VR room. The reported times indicate that most students stayed in the room long enough to watch all the videos. One of the main goals of this room was to learn about the ideal size of a virtual space for classroom activities. When asked about the size of the room in relation to its purpose, unlike the previous room, no participants responded with "large" or "too large". Twelve students (75%) responded with "just right". Only one student (6%) responded that the room was "small", and three students (18%) responded that the room was "a bit small". Three students also mentioned the size of the room in their responses about what they liked most about it. One participant responded, "*It was good to walk around and not too big*." Another participant commented, "The room was *small, which was good for exploring the video material*." Only a few students mentioned disliking the size of the room. For example, one student commented, "*Some areas felt a bit cramped*." Overall, these results were very positive, suggesting that students prefer smaller rooms over larger spaces. It is also noteworthy that, unlike the UNSDG room, no participants reported feeling emptiness or loneliness.

Another aspect highlighted by students was the overall design and details of the room. Approximately one-third of the students praised the museum or art exhibition hall style. Students also mentioned the lighting, layout, and overall aesthetics as what they liked most about the room. One student commented, "*I liked that everything looked and felt like being in a museum, watching various videos with people*." The fact that three participants reported disliking nothing about this room supports the overall positive feedback. However, at least one aspect stood out. Four participants (25%) complained that the sound interfered with each other when more than one video was playing. This was directly related to the fact that the videos were placed close to each other, but it was hoped that fine-tuning the spatial audio configuration of the environment in future iterations could resolve this issue. This would allow for maintaining the distance between videos (without requiring a larger space) and adjusting each video's audio to prevent interference.

**5.2.4. Researcher Observations**

Researchers participated in the virtual space during the MH sessions, walking among students and observing them in the room. The effort to have more students share the same room was successful and created more opportunities for students to interact and communicate. For example, one of the two identical rooms reached its maximum capacity during a session. By having multiple students use the same room simultaneously, we were able to observe students' behavior and communication patterns within the environment in more detail. Regarding students' avatar usage, 75% of participants chose not to customize their avatars, opting for default robot-like avatars. Among the students who chose to customize their avatars, one selected an avatar represented as a panda, while others chose avatars that did not have animal or human-like features. For example, two participants chose drone-shaped avatars as their virtual representations (see [Figure 3F](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F3)).

Observations of student behavior revealed that over 50% of students spent time reading the room's instructions upon their initial entry. Placing instructions in a separate room from the main video-playing room did not distract students, which seemed to contribute to their focus on the educational slides. We observed students forming groups of 3-5 to watch videos. The fact that students formed groups was relevant and very important to our goal of fostering collaboration and communication. By forming groups and moving closer to each other, opportunities for meeting and interacting arose, which was essential for developing a virtual environment that could support and facilitate team formation. As these groups formed, notable behaviors were observed. When students were already watching a video, newcomers either joined the group or waited from a distance to start watching the video from the beginning. It was an exciting moment as some students communicated and interacted to decide whether to wait or continue watching with the group. In some cases, a student joined a group, and the group agreed to restart the video to include the new student. We noted that when asking questions about a specific video, students typically approached the group watching the video and used their voice to ask the question. However, when making general comments about the video or room, or asking general questions, they typed messages into the chat. For example, students used text chat to ask about the duration of the event or to comment on a specific aspect of one of the videos. Although communication among students was not frequent, these behaviors suggest that students were not just interacting using both communication channels (voice and text) available in Mozilla Hubs, but rather using them for different purposes. The voice channel was used for one-on-one conversations or discussions with small groups, while the text channel was used for communicating with the entire group (in this case, for more general topics or opinions). The next room we developed would support team formation for students' major projects. Based on student responses and observations, we gained confidence in the layout and overall appearance of the Pioneers of VR room as a template for new rooms. However, there were also areas for improvement. One of the changes requested by students through the survey was to adjust the audio of the videos to prevent interference. Furthermore, we realized that simply walking among students had limitations in fully understanding how groups formed and how students communicated, and we recognized that this could also influence student behavior. The next section describes the space we developed to facilitate team formation and the strategies we implemented to analyze the results of our efforts.

**6. Room3 - Seekers Room**

For the main group project in the VR4SG course, students (solvers) had to choose one of eight problems proposed by University of Florida community members (seekers) and form their own teams of 5-6 students. The problem proposals were initially presented to students via a Zoom lecture. In this lecture, each seeker presented their proposal to the entire class, and students had the opportunity to interact with the seekers and ask questions. The Seekers room was created to help students learn about the problem proposals and form teams. [Figure 6](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F6) shows the Seekers room. This room can be accessed via the following link ( [https://hubs.mozilla.com/SbF9uYS/call-to-action-room-1](https://hubs.mozilla.com/SbF9uYS/call-to-action-room-1)).

{{IMG:6}}
                                
**Figure 6**. Seekers Room. **(A)** Room 1, containing problem proposals 1 through 4. This room is filled with text comments and other objects added by students. **(B)** Details of a problem proposed by the Athletics Association, surrounded by multiple comments left by students, along with the problem number, text summary, and video. **(C)** Room 2. Only a few students visited Room 2, reflected by the low number of comments.

**6.1. Goals and Challenges**

While the UNSDG room focused on content creation and the Pioneers of VR room on content visualization, this room adopted a hybrid approach. Students' task was to review problem proposals by watching videos in the room and leave written messages to each other explaining why they wanted to form a team to solve a given problem proposal and their opinions. In addition to leaving text messages, students could add other content, such as 3D objects, documents, or web pages, to the virtual environment to motivate others to work on the same project and form teams. While encouraging students to interact, the instructors were careful not to make any comments about how students should behave or communicate while in the room, so as not to influence their behavior and communication patterns.

**6.2. Room Design**

The Seeker Room design is based on the VR Pioneer Room. Considering the similarity of the tasks and positive student feedback, we decided to maintain the overall layout. We adopted the same L-shaped room design for the small room where students read a series of instructions and begin interacting, and for the main space. The details and lighting levels of the Seeker Room are similar to those of the VR Pioneer Room. Each video was placed as if hanging on a wall, and a text description summarizing the problem proposal was added to one side of the video. We implemented several changes to enhance our understanding of student behavior and communication patterns and to address issues raised by students in previous classrooms. Spatial sound was adjusted so that the sound from one video would not interfere with the sound from another. We reduced the audible distance (the distance at which video sound can be heard) to create a space where only minimal sound from the videos could be detected. As a 'quiet' space, we anticipated it would motivate more people to communicate verbally with each other. Regarding size, most students responded that the previous room was "just the right size," while some responded that it was "a bit small." The Seeker Room had one more video than the Pioneer Room, and students were invited to add comments for each video, so we wanted to ensure more space between videos. Therefore, instead of creating two identical rooms for all videos, we decided to create two rooms (Room 1 and Room 2) to display four videos in each room. The rooms were connected to allow students to move freely between them. This approach kept the number and size of the rooms the same (compared to the VR Pioneer Room) but provided more space to present each problem proposal and more room for students to contribute with comments and other content. For observing students, instead of walking around them as we did in the previous room, we decided to observe from a higher vantage point [(MacKenzie, 2012](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B36)). From a higher position, researchers could better observe how students navigated the room and formed groups in that space, which was an approach to the 'wild' [(Chamberlain et al., 2012](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B11)). To achieve this, the ceiling of the room was removed, and a blue sky was added to the scene to give a more natural feel. By removing the ceiling, researchers could position themselves in one corner of the room, standing on top of the wall, to observe the entire room. [Figures 7a, b](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F7) show the researchers' view of the room. We also hypothesized that removing the ceiling would make participants perceive the room as larger.

{{IMG:7}}
                                
                            **Figure 7**. Students interacting in the Seeker Room with multiple avatars implemented. **(A)** Students in Room 1 and **(B)** Room 2 during the 15-minute mark of the session. 14 students are in Room 1, and only 2 are in Room 2. **(C)** Students interacting in groups, circled. Names are blurred for anonymity.

**6.3. Student Survey Responses**

As with the other rooms, all participants reported accessing the Seeker Room via a desktop or laptop. Videos in Rooms 1 and 2 were, on average, approximately 6.5 minutes long (SD = 2.23 minutes). Since four projects were displayed in each room, watching all videos would take just over 52 minutes. Of the 15 respondents, 5 (33.3%) reported spending up to 20 minutes, 7 (46.6%) between 20 and 40 minutes, and 3 over 40 minutes. These reported times indicate that most students did not watch all videos while in the MH room but focused on leaving comments and interacting with other students. This behavior can be partly explained by the text summaries added to each video, which allowed students to quickly recall the details of the problem proposals without watching the videos. Similar to choices in previous rooms, avatar selection did not seem to reflect real-world self-images. Only two students chose to create avatars based on their own faces (using readyplayer.me as described in Section 4.3). All other participants chose avatars such as robots, animals, or other cartoon-like figures. Two students mentioned changing their avatars each time they entered the room. One participant who chose a drone-shaped avatar said they chose it "because it was fun," while another chose a standard avatar "because they didn't want to stand out." [Figure 7](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F7) shows Rooms 1 and 2 filled with students and the various avatars they chose. When asked about the size of the room relative to its purpose, most participants (9, 60%) responded that it was "just the right size," while the remaining 40% felt it was "small" or "a bit small." Several survey participants responded that they found the room too "cluttered" due to too many messages and objects left by other students. This may have led more students to perceive the Seeker Room as smaller compared to their perception of the VR Pioneer Room. These responses and comments suggest that a slightly larger room with more space for students to leave comments would have been a better choice. It is unclear whether removing the ceiling helped students perceive the room as larger, as there were no comments on this aspect. More research is needed to confirm this aspect. Regarding sound conflicts between videos, only one participant mentioned sound as an issue in this classroom, suggesting that adjusting Mozilla Hub's spatial audio settings resolved this problem. Regarding the videos being accompanied by summary text, three students said they liked being able to easily choose among the presented problems. One student commented, "*The text was quick to read, but the videos provided more in-depth information if desired.*"

**6.4. Researcher Observations**

To facilitate richer observations, MH sessions conducted in the Seeker Room were recorded. A total of 75 minutes of video recordings were obtained. [Figures 7a, b](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F7) show the points in time when researchers observed and recorded. For analysis, snapshots were taken at the end of every minute of the video, generating 75 snapshots that were analyzed individually. Researchers annotated these to identify the number of students in each room, their relative positions to each other, when groups formed, and general behaviors in navigating the room. Next, one of the researchers who walked around and observed the room during the session describes student attendance, behavior, and communication patterns based on video recordings and observations [(MacKenzie, 2012](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B36)).

**6.4.1. Student Attendance**

Room 1, containing problem proposals 1-4, consistently had more students than Room 2 (containing projects 5-8). During the 75-minute session, Room 1 had an average of 8.8 participants (SD = 3.46, max = 14), while Room 2 had an average of only 2.4 participants (SD = 1.93, max = 6). Not only were there fewer participants, but Room 1 was never empty during the session (min = 3 participants), whereas Room 2 was empty for 15 minutes out of the 75 minutes and received its first participant 10 minutes after the session began. Furthermore, all students (26) who visited MH during the 75-minute session visited Room 1, while only 17 students visited Room 2. [Figure 8](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F8) shows the number of participants per room per minute during the MH session.

{{IMG:8}}
                                
                            **Figure 8**. Seeker Room: Attendance over time. In the 75-minute session in the Seeker Room, Room 1 consistently had more students than Room 2.

**6.4.2. Student Behavior in the Classroom**

When first entering the room, student behavior showed a similar pattern to the pioneer group in the VR room. Most participants went through three stages: (1) reading the instructions, (2) briefly looking around the room to understand the overall layout, and (3) returning to the first problem statement to begin reviewing them sequentially. Since students were asked to leave comments in text in an environment similar to that shown in [Figure 6b](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#F6), several students left comments after reading the video summaries. In total, participants left 30 comments across Room 1 and Room 2, with most comments left in Room 1. Each project in Room 1 received an average of 5.25 comments (SD = 2.21), while each project in Room 2 received only an average of 2.5 comments (SD = 2.4). The number of comments left directly correlated with the time students spent in each room. Most students visited and stayed longer in Room 1, and this room's projects received the most comments. Several students mentioned that comments left by other students in the room helped them choose whom to form a team with. One possible reason for this could be that students were able to learn about common interests and motivations even if the message author was not present in the room. Similar to the behavior in the previous room, students gathered around the videos. However, this time, students formed groups close to the videos and began playing them. While the videos were playing, students talked about them and remained in their respective groups even after the videos ended, sharing information about their interests. To analyze the impact of the Seekers Room on team formation, we compared the teams formed after the MH session with the groups of students who directly interacted in the Seekers Room. As a result, we found a clear correlation between the formed teams and the groups that interacted in this room. Eight teams were formed, each consisting of 5-6 participants. Of these, two teams (Team 1 and Team 4) were composed of at least four students who spent more than 5 minutes in the room, discussing in a group about the problem proposals they chose to work on. Another three teams (Team 3, Team 6, and Team 8) had three team members who spent time together in the room and later formed a team, while Team 2 had only two team members who spent time together in the MH room. The only exception was Team 7, where none of the students who formed the team interacted in the virtual space. This strongly suggests that the virtual meeting room was successful in helping students form teams on their own. Student feedback also supports this conviction. One student commented, "This room *helped me meet people with similar visions for the proposed problems and form a team*." Another student noted, "*It was good to talk to several people and find out who I wanted to solve which problems with*." One particular behavior was surprising. After exploring the room and interacting for a considerable time, some students appeared to remain stationary in the room. Several students who responded to the survey mentioned that even after achieving their goals in the room, they did not disconnect but remained in the room doing other tasks. One student said they occasionally glanced at the meeting room, while another mentioned continuously listening in and monitoring for interesting conversations. As a result, this behavior led to the room being filled with avatars that were not moving or interacting. While this might seem like strange or negative behavior, it also implies that students were interested enough to remain engaged in the MH session, even if only partially attentive.

**6.4.3. Communication Habits**

Video analysis showed that students interacted more frequently and for longer durations than in previous rooms. This behavior might be due to the nature of the task (interaction for team formation) or because students had become more familiar with the platform. The communication patterns in this room were similar to those observed in previous rooms. Students used voice for small groups or one-on-one conversations and the text channel when communicating with all students in the room. In this room, this communication strategy was demonstrated when several students used text to ask questions about how to move from one room to another or about the next steps for team formation (immediately after the MH room session ended). Additionally, the instructor used the text channel to make announcements because it was more effective than using voice when communicating with all students. Overall, in this room, it was confirmed that students used both voice and text communication channels, choosing one over the other depending on the target group for the message. Text was used to communicate with all students in the room, while voice was used for small groups or one-on-one conversations.

**7. Discussion**

**Spatial Design.** Immersive 3D virtual social spaces (e.g., those developed with the Mozilla Hubs platform), where exploration and communication occur through avatars, offer many opportunities and challenges for developers (or architects) creating spaces different from the real world. For example, both large and small spaces can be created at the same 'cost,' and creating spaces similar to real environments or developing spaces completely detached from reality are equally viable options. The affordances of the virtual world also vary from those of the real world. For instance, in Mozilla Hubs, flying is as natural as walking. While exploring virtual spaces to support team formation in the VR4SG course, we developed several activities and created multiple rooms with various designs. The basic tutorial room and the UNSDG room were based on a wide, open-plan space design that was not created to resemble real-world scenarios. Subsequent rooms adopted a different language. The VR Pioneer Room and the Seeker Room adopted a more familiar design, similar to real-world museums or art exhibition halls. These rooms had walls, were relatively small, and were rich in details such as lighting, furniture, and decorations, making them more akin to the real world. The design of these rooms was primarily based on student feedback and researcher observations. Students reported on room size, details, and preferred overall layout. As a result, rooms with characteristics more similar to the real world received the most responses describing them as 'cozy,' 'comfortable,' and 'appropriately sized for the purpose.' In contrast, the UNSDG room received the most responses describing it as "cold," "empty," and "too large." These results suggest that while the possibilities for designing virtual worlds and spaces are endless, users (undergraduate and graduate students at the University of Florida, USA) prefer virtual space designs with characteristics related to 'comfort' or 'coziness,' similar to the real world. However, further research is needed to fully understand the motivations behind these preferences. Increased exposure and familiarity with avatar-based virtual environments may influence user preferences for spatial design in the long term, and it can also be hypothesized that cultural background plays a significant role in these preferences. Several researchers have explored VR as a tool to elicit user preferences in the real world. Researchers have used VR to derive preferences for hotel room designs ([Bogicevic et al., 2018](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B7)), to identify lighting preferences in office spaces ([Heydarian et al., 2015](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B26)), and to investigate student preferences for learning spaces ([Applegate, 2009](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B2)). However, there are few instances of research investigating user preferences related to virtual space design, especially for social spaces aimed at team formation. In this paper, we have compiled research findings and observations that elicited student preferences into a set of design guidelines to support future developers of virtual 3D social spaces. However, we believe these guidelines can also evolve as researchers gain a greater understanding of the capabilities of social VR platforms and explore their unique affordances. One area requiring further research relates to the fact that we did not fully explore the unique affordances of the MH environment (e.g., flying or teleportation capabilities). Additional research is needed to explore the impact that the unique affordances of virtual environments can have on spatial design and user preferences.

**Student Presence**

In addition to classroom design, we also analyzed participant attendance in virtual spaces. Surprisingly, we found that the name of a room can significantly impact attendance. For example, examining the attendance patterns in the Seeker rooms in Section 6.4.1, most students entered Room 1 to begin interactions and stayed longer compared to Room 2. This attendance pattern was surprising because there were no significant differences between the two rooms or their content. The rooms were numbered randomly, and the problems presented in the rooms were also distributed randomly. The students' choice to visit Room 1 first can be partially explained by the fact that naming the rooms 'Room 1' and 'Room 2' naturally imparts a sense of order, which may have led students to visit Room 1 first. The choice of most students to stay together in Room 1 can be explained by the fact that to form effective teams and engage in collaborative work, individuals need to identify others with similar preferences and shared background knowledge, and preferably associate with them [(Curry and Dunbar, 2013)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B12)). For these reasons, students may have been drawn to rooms where they were more likely to identify similarities among potential team members, leading them to choose to stay longer in the room with the most people. This is further supported by the fact that some students who started their sessions in Room 1 visited Room 2 for a few minutes, and upon finding it empty, returned to Room 1 for the remainder of the time. It seems that using names that do not imply order or sequence would be a better approach for better distributing students across rooms. This would prevent students from being biased when choosing which room to enter if multiple rooms are available. Another approach is to assign a balanced number of students to each room at the beginning of an MH session.

**Avatar Selection**

In a tutorial on avatar customization, the instructor emphasized to students the importance of avatars as self-expression in virtual environments. Previous research suggests that avatar characteristics can enhance or limit people's ability for self-expression [(Nowak and Fox, 2018)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B46)). Therefore, we hypothesized that students would customize their avatars and choose avatars that could convey their potential as good team members to others. However, survey responses and observations indicated that very few students customized their avatars. Most students viewed the opportunity to choose from existing avatars as a chance for 'fun' and opted to use cartoon-like or non-human avatars that did not represent their real-world appearance at all. While this avatar choice could be interpreted as a lost opportunity to support team formation, it can also be viewed from a more positive perspective. Some students reported that avatars complemented their experience in the virtual world. Some students mentioned that, in addition to making the virtual world more 'fun,' choosing and interacting with different avatars was an expected part of the experience. Thus, students may have been more engaged and motivated in the MH sessions than if strict rules had been applied to avatar selection. However, more research is needed to further evaluate avatar selection and its impact on student attendance.

**Communication Channels**

The fact that students used two different communication channels for different purposes greatly contributes to our understanding of how to facilitate communication in these virtual environments. One important aspect is that dual communication channels (simultaneous use of voice and text) represent an affordance that does not exist in the real world. Nevertheless, users appear to quickly recognize and embrace this affordance in their interactions within virtual environments. It is also noteworthy that the behavior of using text for group communication and voice for one-on-one or small group communication emerged organically, meaning no instructions or guidelines were provided to motivate these behaviors. Further research is needed to understand how best to utilize these two communication channels in a classroom setting. Additionally, typing while wearing an HMD is generally impossible or quite cumbersome, so more research is needed to understand the impact of HMD use on interaction and communication [(Boustila et al.](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B8), 2019; [Menzner et al., 2019](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B40)). While HMDs can enhance presence and immersion [(Kwon, 2019)](https://www.frontiersin.org/articles/10.3389/frvir.2021.647801/full?fbclid=IwAR3hq1j_5Enlo6_eD6-nWXgFXVSc2C5WkZYsd3RuVmYPPQbP7F2UWNW0qrs#B35)), they can limit users' communication abilities and may also restrict the time students spend in virtual spaces.

**7.1. Design Considerations**

This section outlines several considerations when designing and developing avatar-based virtual 3D social spaces and activities, reflecting lessons learned from conceptualizing, evaluating, and critiquing the three rooms described in this manuscript for the VR4SG course. While these trends often reinforce existing literature on group interaction and communication, they are important points to note here as frequently observed trends in avatar-based virtual environments.

- **Activity Duration**.
Set limits on the time students spend in virtual rooms for activities. Focusing on engagement increases the likelihood of students meeting and interacting in the virtual environment.

- **Classroom Size**.
Develop smaller rooms rather than large ones for student activities. While size can vary depending on the purpose of the virtual environment, and more research is needed to better understand the relationship between classroom size and tasks performed, smaller classrooms were preferred over larger spaces.
