Smaller rooms eliminated the need for students to walk long distances, thus increasing opportunities for students to communicate and interact in close proximity.

- **Room Layout**.
Develop virtual rooms with layouts similar to real-world spaces. Also, develop closed rooms with doors and walls instead of open spaces. Students reported feeling more comfortable in rooms separated by walls and found it easier to orient themselves and understand the room's layout.

- **Level of Detail**.
Observations showed that students preferred rooms with a high level of detail. Rooms with these characteristics were associated with being "warmer" and "more comfortable," so it is recommended to create rooms with furniture, lighting, and decorations (e.g., paintings on the walls). Rooms with a low level of detail were associated with feeling "cold" and were generally less preferred.

**- Number of Rooms.**
The MH platform limits the number of concurrent users in a single room to 24. Therefore, it is often necessary to create multiple rooms to accommodate all students. To maximize opportunities for students to meet, interact, and communicate, it is recommended to create the minimum number of rooms possible. Additionally, it is advisable to create rooms with complementary content rather than duplicates of rooms with identical content.

**8. Conclusion and Future Work**

This white paper describes efforts to develop and apply avatar-based virtual 3D social spaces created with the Mozilla Hubs platform to complement Zoom lectures when teaching the VR4SG course online. Activities were presented to prepare students to access the MH platform, customize avatars, and develop for the MH platform. We report on iterative efforts to develop three rooms that facilitate interaction and communication. In the UNSDG Room, students created content about a United Nations Sustainable Development Goal of their choice. In the Pioneers of Virtual Reality Room, students consumed video content related to the history of virtual reality. Finally, in the Explorers Room, students learned about problem proposals and consumed and created content to help them form teams and carry out group projects. We observed and surveyed students on aspects such as their perceptions of each room, room size, level of detail, and overall impression. We also observed how students interacted in the classroom and their communication patterns. We found that students preferred highly detailed, enclosed spaces similar to real-world rooms. They also preferred smaller rooms over larger ones. Regarding interaction and communication, smaller classrooms created more opportunities for students to meet and communicate with each other. Similarly, sessions or events lasting a short time (about one hour) focused student attendance and facilitated interaction. In terms of communication, we observed clear patterns regarding the use of voice and text. Students formed circles and conversed in groups, similar to the real world, using voice in the virtual space. However, to communicate with all participants in the room, students often used text. Overall, our efforts to help students form their own teams were successful. Ultimately, 7 out of 8 teams formed to work on this class project met in the virtual space to discuss their chosen problem proposals. Overall, this work illuminated several aspects related to avatar use and customization, in addition to user interaction and communication patterns in virtual 3D social spaces. We discuss aspects related to classroom design, student attendance, avatar selection, and the communication channels used. We also present a set of design considerations for 3D virtual environments that will help develop virtual spaces and future activities focused on facilitating student interaction and communication.

We identified several limitations in this study. We acknowledge that the small number of students who responded to the survey limits the representativeness of the responses. Additionally, all students who responded to the survey were male and used a desktop or laptop. The limited number of participants may be related to the fact that the participation incentive was a small percentage of their grade. Since most students had excellent grades and several other extra credit opportunities, responding to the survey may not have seemed attractive. In future work, we will strive to provide more attractive incentives to encourage greater student participation. Furthermore, it would be valuable to examine the findings with female and mixed groups, as there may be gender-based preferences and behaviors that this study could not ascertain. We also acknowledge that the experimenters observing students in the lab may have introduced bias into the results. However, we made efforts to minimize our influence in the environment, such as maintaining distance from students and assuming unobtrusive avatars.

In future research, we plan to evaluate cultural and gender-related aspects in users' perceptions of virtual environments and further investigate existing real-world architectural and spatial organization theories for virtual space development. We will also investigate the impact that other devices, such as smartphones and HMDs, may have on user behavior and communication patterns in avatar-based virtual environments built to facilitate learning.

The raw data supporting the conclusions of this article will be made available by the authors, without undue reservation.

This study involving human participants was reviewed and approved by the Institutional Review Board of the University of Florida. The patients/participants provided their written informed consent to participate in this study.

AG, JS, and PF-G conceptualized the survey. AG and JS conducted the observations. AS performed the data analysis and wrote the manuscript in consultation with PF-G, JS, and BL. All authors contributed to the conceptualization of the paper.
